An Exploration of Left-Corner Transformations
Andreas Opedal, Eleftheria Tsipidi, Tiago Pimentel, Ryan Cotterell, Tim Vieira

Abstract
The left-corner transformation ( Rosenkrantz and Lewis 1970 ) is used to remove left recursion from context-free grammars, which is an important step towards making the grammar parsable top-down with simple techniques. This paper generalizes prior left-corner transformations to support semiring-weighted production rules and to provide finer-grained control over which left corners may be moved. Our generalized left-corner transformation (GLCT) arose from unifying the left-corner transformation and speculation transformation ( Eisner and Blatz 2007 ) , originally for logic programming. Our new transformation and speculation define equivalent weighted languages. Yet, their derivation trees are structurally different in an important way: GLCT replaces left recursion with right recursion, and speculation does not. We also provide several technical results regarding the formal relationships between the outputs of GLCT, speculation, and the original grammar. Lastly, we empirically investigate the efficiency of GLCT for left-recursion elimination from grammars of nine languages. https://github.com/rycolab/left-corner



1
Introduction

Grammar transformations are functions that map one context-free grammar to another.
The formal language theory literature contains numerous examples of such transformations, including nullary rule removal, rule binarization, and conversion to normal forms, e.g., those of
Chomsky 1959
and
Greibach 1965
. In this work, we study and generalize the left-corner transformation
(
Rosenkrantz and Lewis 1970
)
. Qualitatively, this transformation maps the derivation trees of an original grammar into isomorphic trees in the transformed grammar. The trees of the transformed grammar will be such that the base subtree of a left-recursive chain (the left corner) is
hoisted
up in a derivation tree while replacing the left-recursive path to the left corner with a right-recursive path to an empty constituent.
Figure

1
provides an example.

{forest}

Figure 1:
Left:
An example derivation containing left-recursive rules.

Right:
The corresponding derivation after our left-corner transformation. Observe that (i) the lower
\mathrm{NP}
subtree has been hoisted up the tree, (ii) the
left
recursion from
\mathrm{NP}
to
\mathrm{NP}
has been replaced with
right
recursion from
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{S}}}{{\mathrm{NP}}}
to
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{S}}}{{\mathrm{NP}}}
.

A common use case of the left-corner transformation is to remove left recursion from a grammar, which is necessary for converting the grammar to Greibach normal form and for several top-down parsing algorithms
(
Aho and Ullman 1972
)
.
As an additional effect, it reduces the stack depth of top-down parsing
(
Johnson 1998
)
, which makes it an interesting method for psycholinguistic applications
(
Roark et al. 2009
;
Charniak 2010
)
. The closely related left-corner
parsing
strategy has been argued to be more cognitively plausible than alternatives due to its constant memory load across both left- and right-branching structures and low degree of local ambiguity for several languages
(
Johnson-Laird 1983
;
Abney and Johnson 1991
;
Resnik 1992
)
; indeed, empirical evidence has shown that certain left-corner parsing steps correlate with brain activity
(
Brennan and Pylkkänen 2017
;
Nelson et al. 2017
)
and reading times
(
Oh et al. 2022
)
.
1
1

1

Moreover, statistical left-corner parsers have proven themselves empirically effective for several grammar formalisms
(
Roark and Johnson 1999
;
Roark 2001
;
Díaz et al. 2002
;
Noji and Miyao 2014
;
Noji et al. 2016
;
Shain et al. 2016
;
Stanojević and Stabler 2018
;
Kitaev and Klein 2020
)
.

This paper uncovers an interesting connection between the left-corner transformation and the speculation transformation
(
Eisner and Blatz 2007
)
.
2
2

2

Speculation was originally a transformation for weighted logic programs, which we have adapted to CFGs.

We show that speculation also hoists subtrees up the derivation tree, as in the left-corner transformation. However, in contrast, it does
not
remove left recursion. In uncovering the similarity to speculation, we discover that speculation has been formulated with more specificity than prior left-corner transformations: it has parameters that allow it to control which types of subtrees are permitted to be hoisted and which paths they may be hoisted along. We bring this flexibility to the left-corner transformation with a novel
generalized
left-corner transformation (GLCT).
3
3

3

We note that generalized left-corner
parsers
also exist
(
Demers 1977
;
Nederhof 1993
)
. However, they are generalized differently to our transformation.
It turns out that the latter functionality is provided by the selective left-corner transformation
(
Johnson and Roark 2000
)
; however, the former is new.

We provide several new technical results:
4
4

4

These technical results fill some important gaps in the literature, as prior work
(
Rosenkrantz and Lewis 1970
;
Johnson 1998
;
Johnson and Roark 2000
;
Moore 2000
;
Eisner and Blatz 2007
)
did not provide formal proofs.

(i)

We prove that GLCT preserves the weighted languages and that an isomorphism between derivation trees exists (
theorem

1
).

(ii)

We provide explicit methods for mapping back and forth between the derivation trees (
algorithms

1
and
2
).

(iii)

We prove that the set of derivation trees for speculation and GLCT are isomorphic (
theorem

2
).

(iv)

We prove that our GLCT-based left-recursion elimination strategy removes left recursion (
theorem

4
).

Additionally, we empirically investigate the efficiency of GLCT for left-recursion elimination from grammars of nine different languages in
section

4.1
.

2
Preliminaries

This section provides the necessary background on the concepts pertaining to semiring-weighted context-free grammars that this paper requires.

Definition 1
.

A
semiring
is a tuple
\left\langle\mathbb{W},\oplus,\otimes,\textbf{0},\textbf{1}\right\rangle
where
\mathbb{W}
is a set and the following hold:

•

\oplus
is an associative and commutative binary operator with an identity element
\textbf{0}\in\mathbb{W}

•

\otimes
is an associative binary operator with an identity element
\textbf{1}\in\mathbb{W}

•

Distributivity:
\forall a,b,c\in\mathbb{W}
,
(a\oplus b)\otimes c\!=\!(a\otimes c)\oplus(b\otimes c)
and
c\otimes(a\oplus b)\!=\!(c\otimes a)\oplus(c\otimes b)

•

Annihilation:
\forall a\in\mathbb{W}
,
a\otimes\textbf{0}\!=\!\textbf{0}\otimes a\!=\!\textbf{0}

The semiring is commutative if
\otimes
is commutative.

We highlight a few commutative semirings and their use cases:

•

boolean
\left\langle\{\bot,\top\},\lor,\land,\bot,\top\right\rangle
: string membership in a language,

•

nonnegative real
\left\langle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\!\cup\!\{\infty\},+,\cdot,0,1\right\rangle
: the total probability of a string,

•

viterbi
\left\langle[0,1],\max,\cdot,1,0\right\rangle
: the weight of the most likely derivation of a string.

For further reading on semirings in NLP, we recommend
Goodman 1999
and
Huang 2008
.

Our work studies weighted context-free grammars (WCFGs), which define a tractable family of weighted languages—called weighted context-free languages—that is frequently used in NLP applications (see, e.g.,
Jurafsky and Martin 2020
).

Definition 2
.

A
weighted context-free grammar
is a tuple
\mathfrak{G}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
where

•

\mathcal{N}
is a set of nonterminal symbols

•

\mathcal{V}
is a set of terminal symbols,
\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{N}=\emptyset

•

\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{N}
is the start symbol

•

\mathcal{R}
is a bag (multiset) of weighted production rules.
Each rule
r\in\mathcal{R}
is of the form
\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
where
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}
,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N})^{*}
, and
w\in\mathbb{W}
.
We assume that
\mathbb{W}
is commutative semiring.

We will use the following notational conventions:

•

\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{Z}\in\mathcal{N}
for nonterminals

•

a,b,c\in\mathcal{V}
for terminals

•

\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{V}^{*}
for a sequence of terminals

•

\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in(\mathcal{V}\!\cup\!\mathcal{N})
for a terminal or nonterminal symbol

•

\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N})^{*}
for a sequence of nonterminals or terminals

•

\mathfrak{G}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime},\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right\rangle
for WCFGs.

We write
w\left(r\right)
to access the weight of a rule
r
.

When describing a grammar and its rules, we may use the following terms: The
size
of a grammar is
\sum_{(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{}\boldsymbol{\alpha})\in\mathcal{R}}(1\!+\!|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|)
. The
arity
of a rule
\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{}\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is equal to
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|
; thus, a rule is
nullary
if
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=0
,
unary
if
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=1
, and so on. For technical reasons, it is often convenient to eliminate nullary rules from the grammar.
5
5

5

Opedal et al. 2023
provides an efficient method to remove nullary rules from semiring-weighted CFGs, which we make use of in our experiments (
section

4.1
).

A
derivation
is a rooted, (
\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}
)-labeled, ordered tree where
each internal node must connect to its children by a production rule.
6
6

6

Note: a derivation may be built by a rule with an empty right-hand side; thus, the leaves may be elements of
\mathcal{N}
. When rendering such a derivation, childlessness is marked with
\varepsilon
.

To access the root
label
of a derivation
\boldsymbol{\delta}
, we write
\ell\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
.
The
set of all derivations
of
\mathfrak{G}
is the smallest set
\mathcal{D}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{V}\,\cup

(1)

The
weight

\omega\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\in\mathbb{W}
of a derivation

\displaystyle\bullet\textbf{ if }\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{V}:\omega\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\textbf{1}

(3)

\displaystyle\bullet\textbf{else: }\omega\left(\!\!\!\hbox to59.27pt{\vbox to55.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.94809pt\lower-48.65256pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{}}{{{
{}{}{}}}}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{

\omega\left(\hbox to12.62pt{\vbox to19.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.30751pt\lower-14.28319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}{{}{{}}}}{}{{{}}{{}{}}

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.7}{0.0}{0.0}{0.7}{-7.27045pt}{-14.28319pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\end{array}

The
weighted language
of
\alpha\in(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N})
is a function

\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha}:\mathcal{V}^{*}\to\mathbb{W}
defined as follows:

\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})}\omega\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)

(6)

where
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\left\{\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}\colon\ell\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)=\alpha\right\}
denotes the subset of
\mathcal{D}
containing trees labeled
\alpha
, and
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\left\{\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}\colon\sigma\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)=\mathbf{x}\right\}
denotes those with yield
\mathbf{x}
. In words, the value of a string
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}^{*}
in the weighted language
\mathfrak{G}_{\alpha}
is the
\oplus
-sum of the weights of all trees in
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}
with
\mathbf{x}
as its yield. The weighted language of the
grammar

\mathfrak{G}
is
\mathfrak{G}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})
. Given a set of symbols
\mathcal{X}
, we write
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}
as shorthand for
\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}
. Lastly, let
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\right\rrbracket
denote the weighted language generated by the tree
\boldsymbol{\delta}
.
7
7

7

Formally,
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\right\rrbracket(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\omega\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\text{ if }\mathbf{x}\!=\!\sigma\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\text{ else }\textbf{0}
.

We define the following operations on weighted languages
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
, for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
:

•

Union:
\left[\mathfrak{G}\oplus\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\right](\mathbf{x})\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\!\mathfrak{G}(\mathbf{x})\oplus\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x})

•

Concatenation:

\left[\mathfrak{G}\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\right](\mathbf{x})\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\!\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{y}\circ\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{x}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{G}(\mathbf{y})\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}(\mathbf{z})

Note that these operations form a (noncommutative) semiring over weighted languages where
0
is the language that assigns weight zero to all strings and

1
is the language that assigns one to the empty string and zero to other strings.

The weighted language of
\mathfrak{G}
may also be expressed as a certain solution
8
8

8

Note that the system of equations does not necessarily have a unique solution. In the case of an
\omega
-continuous semiring,
eq.

6
coincides with the
smallest
solution to
eq.

7
under the natural ordering
(
Droste and Kuich 2009
)
.

to the following system of equations:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{a}=\textbf{1}_{a}

\displaystyle\forall a\in\mathcal{V}

(7a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\mathrm{X}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{K})\in\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}w\circ\mathfrak{G}_{\beta_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{G}_{\beta_{K}}

\displaystyle\forall\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}

(7b)

where
\textbf{1}_{a}
is the weighted language that assigns
1
to the string
a
and
0
to other strings.

We say that
\alpha
is
useless
if there does not exist a derivation
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}
that has a subderivation
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\prime}
with
\ell\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\prime}\right)\!=\!\alpha
. We define
trimming

\textsc{trim}(\mathfrak{G})
as removing each useless nonterminal and any rule in which they participate. It is easy to see that trimming does not change the weighted language of the grammar because no useless nonterminals participate in a derivation rooted at
\mathcal{S}
. We can trim useless rules in linear time using well-known algorithms
(
Hopcroft and Ullman 1979
)
.

We say that grammars
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
are
equal
(
\mathfrak{G}\!=\!\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
) if they have the same tuple representation after trimming. We say they are
equivalent
(
\mathfrak{G}\!\equiv\!\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
) if they define the same weighted language.
9
9

9

I.e.,
(\mathfrak{G}\!\equiv\!\mathfrak{G}^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow\forall\mathbf{x}\in(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{V}^{\prime})^{*}\colon\mathfrak{G}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})
.

We say that they are
\mathcal{X}
-bijectively equivalent
(
\mathfrak{G}\!\equiv_{\mathcal{X}}\!\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
) if a structure-preserving bijection of type
{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{X}}
exists. The mapping
{\phi}
is
structure-preserving
if (
\forall\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}
) it is
(i) label-preserving (
\ell\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\!=\!\ell\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)
),
(ii) yield-preserving (
\sigma\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\!=\!\sigma\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)
), and
(iii) weight-preserving (
\omega\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\!=\!\omega\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)
).
Suppose
\mathfrak{G}\equiv_{\mathcal{X}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}\!=\!\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, then
\mathfrak{G}\!\equiv\!\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
, but not conversely.
A benefit of this stronger notion of equivalence is that derivations in
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
are interconvertible: we can parse in
\mathfrak{G}
and convert to a parse in
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
and vice versa, assuming
\mathcal{S}\!=\!\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{X}
.
10
10

10

Under these conditions, our
\mathcal{X}
-bijective equivalence notion becomes a weighted extension of what
Gray and Harrison 1969
call
complete covers
and
Nijholt 1980
calls
proper covers
. However, their definitions assume traditional string-rewriting derivations instead of tree-structured derivations.

3
Transformations

This section specifies our novel generalized left-corner transformation, its correctness guarantees, and its connections to prior left-corner transformations. We also describe the speculation transformation
(
Eisner and Blatz 2007
)
and discuss the connections between the speculation transformation and our generalized left-corner transformation.

3.1
Generalized Left-Corner Transformation

This section introduces the generalized left-corner transformation (GLCT).
11
11

11

A generalized
right
-corner transformation can be defined analogously—applications of such a transformation are given in
Schuler et al. 2010
and
Amini and Cotterell 2022
.
This transformation extends prior left-corner transformations by providing additional parameters that control which subtrees can be hoisted.

Definition 3
.

The
generalized left-corner transformation

\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
takes as input

•

a grammar
\mathfrak{G}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle

•

a subset of (non-nullary) rules
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
(called left-corner recognition rules)

•

a subset of symbols
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N})
(called left-corner recognition symbols)

and outputs a grammar
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime},\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right\rangle
with

•

a superset of nonterminals (
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{N}
)
12
12

12

\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\!=\!\mathcal{N}\cup\{\widetilde{X}\mid\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}\}\cup\{\nicefrac{{\alpha}}{{\beta}}\mid\alpha,\beta\in(\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V})\}

•

the same set of terminals (
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\!=\!\mathcal{V}
)

•

the same start symbol (
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\!=\!\mathcal{S}
)

•

the weighted production rules (
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}\!\setminus\!\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(8a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\alpha}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N},{\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}}

(8b)

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\varepsilon\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N}

(8c)

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\beta}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathrm{Y}\in\mathcal{N}

(8d)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{R}\!\setminus\!\overline{\mathcal{R}}

(8e)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\xrightarrow{w}\widetilde{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\alpha\notin\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(8f)

The transformation creates two kinds of new nonterminals using
\widetilde{\alpha}
and
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}
.
13
13

13

This notation works as follows: we associate a unique identifier
\mathit{id}
with the transformation instance. Then,
\widetilde{\alpha}\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\!\left\langle\mathit{id},\alpha\right\rangle\text{ if }\alpha\in\mathcal{N}\text{ else }\alpha
and
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\!\left\langle\mathit{id},\mathrm{X},\alpha\right\rangle
. This ensures that the symbols produced cannot conflict with those on
\mathcal{N}
(i.e.,
\forall\alpha\in\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}
,
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}\colon\widetilde{\alpha},\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}\notin\mathcal{N}
).

In
3
, we see that GLCT introduces new nonterminals of two varieties:
slashed
nonterminals (denoted
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\alpha}}
) and
frozen
14
14

14

Our notion of frozen nonterminals is borrowed directly from the speculation transformation
(
Eisner and Blatz 2007
)
, where they are called
other
.

nonterminals (denoted
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}
). The frozen and slashed nonterminals are each defined recursively.

•

Slashed nonterminals are built by a base case
eq.

8c
and a recursive case
eq.

8d
.

•

Frozen nonterminals are built by a base case
eq.

8e
and a recursive case
eq.

8f
.

We see in
eq.

8a
and
eq.

8b
that GLCT replaces the rules defining the
original
nonterminals (
\mathcal{N}
) with rules that use GLCT’s new nonterminals; the only way to build a nonterminal from
\mathcal{N}
is using one of these two rules. We refer to these as
recovery rules
because they recover the original symbols from the new frozen and slashed symbols. We also see that
eq.

8d
is responsible for converting left recursion into right recursion because the slashed nonterminal on its right-hand side is moved to the right of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
. We will return to this when we discuss speculation in
section

3.2
.
fig.

2
illustrates how GLCT transforms trees.

Left corner and spine.

To better understand the parameters
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, we define the spine and left corner of a derivation
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}
of the original grammar.
Suppose
\boldsymbol{\delta}
has the following general form:
\gamma^{(K)}
\gamma^{(K-1)}

\gamma^{(2)}
\gamma^{(1)}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-2})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})}
ab
\cdots

Then, we define the
spine

\zeta\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
as the maximum-length sequence of rules
(\gamma^{(K)}\to\gamma^{(K-1)}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})})\cdots(\gamma^{(2)}\to\gamma^{(1)}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})})
along the left edges of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
where each rule is in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. The
left corner

\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
of a tree
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}
is the bottommost subtree
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}
of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
with
\ell\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\right)\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}
that is reachable starting at the root
\ell\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
along the left edges of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
where each edge comes from a rule in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. If no such subtree exists, we say that
\boldsymbol{\delta}
has no left corner and write
\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\!=\!\bot
. We write
\boldsymbol{\delta}/\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
to denote the
\boldsymbol{\delta}
with
\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
replaced by the empty subtree; we define
\boldsymbol{\delta}/\bot\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\boldsymbol{\delta}
. Lastly, we define
D/\alpha\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\left\{\boldsymbol{\delta}/\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\ \middle|\ \boldsymbol{\delta}\in D,\ell\left(\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)=\alpha\right\}
where
D
is a set of derivations and
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}\cup\{\bot\}
.

To illustrate, let
\boldsymbol{\delta}
be the derivation on the left in
fig.

1
. The right-hand side derivation results from applying GLCT with
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\!=\!\{\mathrm{NP}\}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\!=\!\allowbreak\{\mathcal{S}\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{NP}\,\mathrm{VP},\allowbreak\mathrm{NP}\rightarrow\mathrm{PossP}\,\mathrm{NN},\allowbreak\mathrm{PossP}\rightarrow\mathrm{NP}\,\text{'}s\}
. The spine of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
, then, is the sequence of rules in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
(in the same order), and its left corner is the lower
\mathrm{NP}
-subtree. Note how the left corner is the subtree hoisted by the transformation.

Interpretation.

The symbol
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}
represents the weighted language of
\mathrm{X}
where we have replaced its left corner subtrees labeled
\alpha
(if one exists) with
\varepsilon
.
We can see that the recovery rule
eq.

8b
uses these slashed nonterminals to reconstruct
\mathrm{X}
by each of its left-corner types (found in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
). We also have a recovery rule
eq.

8a
that uses a frozen nonterminal
\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}
, which represents the other ways to build
\mathrm{X}
(i.e., those that do not have a left corner in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
). Thus, the weighted language of
\mathrm{X}
decomposes as a certain sum of slashed nonterminals and its frozen version (formalized below).

Proposition 1

(Decomposition)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
. Then, for any
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\mathrm{X}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}}

(9)

See
appendix

A
for proof. Next, we describe the weighted languages of the slashed and frozen nonterminals in relation to the derivations in the original grammar.
proposition

2
establishes that the weighted language of
\widetilde{\alpha}
is the total weight of
\alpha
derivations without a left corner, and
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}
is the total weight of all
\mathrm{X}
derivations with an
\alpha
left corner that has been replaced by
\varepsilon
.

Proposition 2

(Weighted language relationship)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{C}},\overline{\mathcal{R}})
.
Then, for any
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}/\bot\end{subarray}}\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\right\rrbracket\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{X}}/\alpha\end{subarray}}\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\delta}}\right\rrbracket

See
appendix

B
for proof.

Special cases.

We now discuss how our transformation relates to prior left-corner transformations. The
basic left-corner transformation

(
Rosenkrantz and Lewis 1970
;
Johnson 1998
)
is
\textsc{lct}(\mathfrak{G})\!=\!\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V})
, i.e., we set
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\!=\!\mathcal{R}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\!=\!\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}
.
15
15

15

That is, the output grammars are equal post trimming. The only useful rules are instances of
eq.

8b
,
eq.

8c
, and
eq.

8d
. Furthermore, the
eq.

8b
rules will be useless unless
\widetilde{\alpha}
is a terminal. With these observations, verifying that GLCT matches
Johnson 1998
’s (
Johnson 1998
) presentation of LCT is straightforward.
This forces the leftmost leaf symbol of the tree to be the left corner, which is either a terminal or the left-hand side of a nullary rule. The
selective left-corner transformation
(SLCT;
Johnson and Roark 2000
)
\textsc{slct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}})\!=\!\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V})
supports left-corner recognition rules
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, but it does not allow control over the left-corner recognition symbols, as
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is required to be
\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}
.
16
16

16

More precisely, we are using the SLCT with top-down factoring
(
Johnson and Roark 2000
, §2.5)
.
Thus, SLCT takes
any
subtree at the bottom of the spine to be its left corner. Frozen nonterminals enable us to restrict the left corners to those labeled
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

Formal guarantees.

We now discuss the formal guarantees related to our transformation in the form of an equivalence theorem (
theorem

1
) and an asymptotic bound on the number of rules in the output grammar (
proposition

3
).
theorem

1
establishes that the GLCT’s output grammar is
\mathcal{N}
-bijectively equivalent to its input grammar.

Theorem 1

(
\mathcal{N}
-bijective equivalence)
.

Suppose
{\mathfrak{G}=}\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
is a WCFG and
{\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}=}\,\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
where
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N}
.
Then,
\mathfrak{G}\equiv_{\mathcal{N}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
.

We prove this theorem in
appendix

C
. To our knowledge, this is the only formal correctness proof for any left-corner transformation.
17
17

17

We note that
Aho and Ullman 1972
prove correctness for an alternative method to remove left recursion, which is used as a first step when converting a grammar to Greibach normal form. This method, however, might lead to an exponential increase in grammar size
(
Moore 2000
)
.

In addition,
appendix

C
provides pseudocode for the derivation mapping
{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
and its inverse
{\phi^{-1}}
, in
algorithms

1
and
2
, respectively.

We can bound the number of rules in the output grammar as a function of the input grammar and the transformation’s parameters
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proposition 3
.

The number of rules in
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
is no more than

|\mathcal{R}|+|\mathcal{N}|\,(1+|\overline{\mathcal{C}}|+|\overline{\mathcal{R}}|)+|\mathcal{N}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}}|+|\mathcal{V}|

Proof.

We bound the maximum number of rules in each rule category of
3
:

|\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:lc-recovery-frozen}|\leq|\mathcal{N}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}}|\qquad\quad

|\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:lc-recovery-slash}|\leq|\mathcal{N}|\,|\overline{\mathcal{C}}|

|\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:lc-slash-base}|\leq|\mathcal{N}|+|\mathcal{V}|

|\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:lc-slash-recursive}|\leq|\overline{\mathcal{R}}|\,|\mathcal{N}|

|\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:lc-frozen-base}|\leq|\mathcal{R}\!\setminus\!\overline{\mathcal{R}}|

|\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:lc-frozen-recursive}|\leq|\overline{\mathcal{R}}|

Each of these bounds can be derived straightforwardly from
3
. Summing them, followed by algebraic simplification, proves
proposition

3
.
∎

The bound in
proposition

3
is often loose in practice, as many of the rules created by the transformation are useless. In
section

4
, we describe how to use GLCT to eliminate left recursion, and we investigate the growth of the transformed grammar for nine natural language grammars in
section

4.1
.

Figure 2:
This figure is a schematic characterization of the one-to-one correspondence of derivations of the original grammar, speculation, and GLCT transformations. The diagram assumes that the rules exposed in the derivation
\boldsymbol{\delta}
form its spine
\zeta\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
. However, (b) and (d) assume that
\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is the left corner
\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
, and (c) assumes that
\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\!=\!\bot
.
Note that the rules in the spine that are below the left corner are frozen. When the left corner is
\bot
(i.e., case (c)), the spines of the GLCT and speculation trees are transformed in the same manner. We note that in each transformation case ((b), (c), or (d)), each
\beta
-labeled subtree is recursively transformed, and its root label is preserved. We see that both speculation (d) and GLCT (b) hoist the same left-corner subtree (i.e., the
\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}
subtree in the diagram) to attach at the top of the new derivation. However, the left recursion is transformed into a right-recursive tree in GLCT. Lastly, we observe that the slashed nonterminals in GLCT have a common numerator (
\gamma^{(K)}
), whereas, in speculation, they have a common denominator (
\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}
).

Optimizations.

We briefly describe two improvements to our method’s practical efficiency.

Reducing the number of useless rules.

For efficiency, we may adapt two filtering strategies from prior work that aim to reduce the number of useless rules created by the transformation.
18
18

18

These strategies are provided in our implementation.

We provide equations for how to modify
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
in GLCT to account for these filters in
appendix

G
.

Fast nullary rule elimination.

Nullary rule elimination is often required as a preprocessing step in parsing applications
(
Aho and Ullman 1972
)
. When eliminating the nullary rules introduced by our transformations (i.e., the base case for slashed rules), there turns out to be a special linear structure that can be exploited for efficiency. We describe the details of this speedup in
appendix

H
.

3.2
Speculation Transformation

In this section, we adapt
Eisner and Blatz 2007
’s (
Eisner and Blatz 2007
; §6.5) speculation transformation from weighted logic programming to WCFGs.
19
19

19

The translation was direct and required essentially no invention on our behalf. However, we have made one aesthetic change to their transformations that we wish to highlight: the closest WCFG interpretation of
Eisner and Blatz 2007
’s (
Eisner and Blatz 2007
) speculation transformation restricts the slashed nonterminals beyond
eq.

11c
and
eq.

11d
; their version constrains the denominator to be
\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. This difference disappears after trimming because
useful
slashed nonterminals must be consumed by the recovery rule
eq.

11b
, which imposes the
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
constraint one level higher. We prefer our version as it enables
theorem

2
, which shows that GLCT and speculation produce
\mathcal{X}
-bijective equivalent grammars for all nonterminals. The pruned version would result in a weaker theorem with
\mathcal{X}
being a
subset
of the nonterminals with a nuanced specification.

We will provide a new interpretation for speculation that does not appear in the literature.
20
20

20

Vieira 2023
’s (
Vieira 2023
) dissertation, which appeared contemporaneously with this paper, adopts our same interpretation.
In particular, we observe that speculation, like the left-corner transformation, is a subtree hoisting transformation.

Definition 4
.

The
speculation transformation

\textsc{spec}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
takes as input

•

a grammar
\mathfrak{G}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle

•

a subset of (non-nullary) rules
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
(called the left-corner recognition rules)

•

a subset of symbols
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N})
(called the left-corner recognition symbols)

and outputs a grammar
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime},\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right\rangle
with

•

a superset of nonterminals (
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{N}
)
21
21

21

See
footnote

12
.

•

the same set of terminals (
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\!=\!\mathcal{V}
)

•

the same start symbol (
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\!=\!\mathcal{S}
)

•

the weighted production rules (
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(11a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\alpha}\,\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N},\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(11b)

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\varepsilon\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N}

(11c)

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\xrightarrow{w}\nicefrac{{\alpha}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\colon

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathrm{Y}\!\in\!\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N}

(11d)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{R}\!\setminus\!\overline{\mathcal{R}}

(11e)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\xrightarrow{w}\widetilde{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\alpha\notin\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(11f)

Upon inspection, we see that the only difference between speculation and GLCT is how they define their slashed nonterminals, as the other rules are identical.
The slashed nonterminals have the same base case
eq.

8c
and
eq.

11c
. However, their recursive cases
eq.

8d
and
eq.

11d
differ in an intriguing way:

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\beta}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathrm{Y}\in\mathcal{N}\ (\ref{eq:lc-slash-recursive})

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\xrightarrow{w}\nicefrac{{\alpha}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\colon

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathrm{Y}\in\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N}\ (\ref{eq:spec-slash-recursive})

This difference is why GLCT can eliminate left recursion and speculation cannot: GLCT’s slashed nonterminal appears to the right of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, and speculation’s appears on the left. For GLCT,
\mathrm{Y}
is passed along the numerator of the slashed nonterminal, whereas, for speculation,
\mathrm{Y}
is passed along the denominator.
theorem

2
(below) establishes that speculation and GLCT are bijectively equivalent for their complete set of nonterminals.
22
22

22

We note that the set of
useful
slashed and frozen nonterminals typically differs between GLCT and speculation.

Theorem 2

(Speculation–GLCT bijective equivalence)
.

For any grammar
\mathfrak{G}
, and choice of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
,
\textsc{spec}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
and
\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
are
\mathcal{X}
-bijectively equivalent where
\mathcal{X}
is the complete set of symbols (i.e., original, frozen, and slashed).

See
appendix

D
for the proof sketch.
We also provide the first proof of equivalence for speculation.

Theorem 3
.

For any grammar
\mathfrak{G}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
, and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N}
:
\textsc{spec}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})\equiv_{\mathcal{N}}\mathfrak{G}
.

Proof.

The theorem follows directly from
theorem

1
,
theorem

2
, and the compositionality of bijective functions.
∎

4
Left-Recursion Elimination

Motivated by the desire for efficient top-down parsing for which left-recursion poses challenges (
section

1
), we describe how GLCT may be used to transform a possibly left-recursive grammar
\mathfrak{G}
into a bijectively equivalent grammar
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
without left-recursion.
23
23

23

Note: when we say left recursion, we often mean
unbounded
left recursion.

The bijective equivalence (
theorem

1
) ensures that we can apply an inverse transformation to the derivation tree of the transformed grammar into its corresponding derivation tree in the original grammar. This section provides an efficient and (provably correct) recipe for left-recursion elimination using GLCT. We experiment with this recipe on natural language grammars in
section

4.1
.

Our left-recursion elimination recipe is based on a single application of GLCT, which appropriately chooses parameters
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. We describe how to determine these parameters by analyzing the structure of the rules in
\mathfrak{G}
.

We define the
left-recursion depth
of a derivation tree
d(\boldsymbol{\delta})
as the length of the path from the root to the leftmost leaf node. The left-recursion depth of a grammar is
d(\mathfrak{G})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}\max_{\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}}d(\boldsymbol{\delta})
. We say that
\mathfrak{G}
is
left-recursive
iff
d(\mathfrak{G})
is unbounded. To analyze whether
\mathfrak{G}
is left-recursive, we can analyze its left-recursion graph, which accurately characterizes the left-recursive paths from the root of a derivation to its leftmost leaf in the set of derivations
\mathcal{D}
. The
left-recursion graph
of the grammar
\mathfrak{G}
is a labeled directed graph
G\!=\!\left\langle N,E\right\rangle
with nodes
N\!=\!\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N}
and edges
E\!=\!\{(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{r}\alpha)\mid r\in\mathcal{R},r\!=\!(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{}\alpha\,{\cdots})\}
. It should be clear that the
\mathfrak{G}
is left-recursive iff
G
has a cyclic subgraph. We classify a rule
r
as left-recursive if the edge labeled
r
is an edge in any cyclic subgraph of
G
. To determine the set of left-recursive rules, we identify the strongly connected components (SCCs) of
G
(e.g., using
Tarjan 1972
’s (
Tarjan 1972
) algorithm).
The SCC analysis returns a function
\pi
that maps each of
G
’s nodes to the identity of its SCC. Then, a rule
r
is left-recursive iff its corresponding edge
\alpha\xrightarrow{r}\beta
satisfies
\pi(\alpha)\!=\!\pi(\beta)
.
24
24

24

Note that nullary rules cannot be left-recursive.
To ensure that left recursion is eliminated,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
must include all left-recursive rules.

We use the following set to provide a sufficient condition on
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
to eliminate left recursion:

\displaystyle\mathrm{bottoms}(\overline{\mathcal{R}})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}

(12)

\displaystyle\qquad(\mathcal{V}\cup\{\mathrm{X}\mid(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{r}\alpha)\in E,r\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{R}}\})

\displaystyle\qquad\cap\{\alpha\mid(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{}\alpha\cdots)\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}\})

This set captures the set of nodes that may appear at the bottom of a spine (for the given
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
). This is because the spine is defined as the longest sequence of rules in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
along the left of a derivation; thus, a spine can end in one of two ways (1) it reaches a terminal, or (2) it encounters a rule outside of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Thus, the bottom elements of the spine are the set of terminals, and the set of nodes with at least one
(\mathcal{R}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{R}})
-labeled outgoing edge—which we refine to nodes that might appear in the spine (i.e., those in the leftmost position of the rules in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
).

With these definitions in place, we can provide sufficient conditions on the GLCT parameter sets that will remove left recursion:

Theorem 4

(Left-recursion elimination)
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\textsc{trim}(\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}}))
where

•

\mathfrak{G}
has no unary rules

•

\overline{\mathcal{R}}\supseteq
the left-recursive rules in
\mathfrak{G}

•

\overline{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq\mathrm{bottoms}(\overline{\mathcal{R}})

Then,
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is not left-recursive. Moreover,
d(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime})\leq 2\!\cdot\!C
where
C
is the number of SCCs in the left-recursion graph for
\mathfrak{G}
.

See
appendix

E
for the proof.

Example.

In
fig.

1
, we made use of
theorem

4
to remove the left-recursion from
\mathrm{NP}
to
\mathrm{NP}
, applying GLCT with
\overline{\mathcal{C}}=\{\mathrm{NP}\}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}=\allowbreak\{\mathcal{S}\xrightarrow{}\mathrm{NP}\,\mathrm{VP},\allowbreak\mathrm{NP}\rightarrow\mathrm{PossP}\,\mathrm{NN},\allowbreak\mathrm{PossP}\rightarrow\mathrm{NP}\;\text{'}s\}
.
25
25

25

Note that omitting
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{NP}\,\mathrm{VP}
from
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
would have also eliminated left recursion in this example, but we would have obtained a different output tree in the figure.

Our recipe.

We take
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
as the set of left-recursive rules in
\mathfrak{G}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
as
\mathrm{bottoms}(\overline{\mathcal{R}})
.
26
26

26

Our choice for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is consistent with the recommendation for SLCT in
Johnson and Roark 2000
.
This minimizes the upper bound given by
proposition

3
subject to the constraints given in
theorem

4
.

Special cases.

theorem

4
implies that the basic left-corner transformation and the selective left-corner transformations (with
\overline{\mathcal{R}}

\supseteq
the left-recursive rules) will eliminate left recursion. Experimentally, we found that our recipe produces a slightly smaller grammar than the selective option (see
section

4.1
).

Unary rules.

The reason
theorem

4
requires that
\mathfrak{G}
is unary-free
is that the left-corner transformation cannot remove unary cycles of this type.
27
27

27

Prior left-corner transformations
(
Johnson and Roark 2000
;
Moore 2000
)
are limited in the same manner.
To see why, note that
\boldsymbol{\beta}\!=\!\varepsilon
for a unary rule
eq.

8d
; thus, the transformed rule will have a slashed nonterminal in its leftmost position, so it may be left-recursive. Fortunately, unary rule cycles can be eliminated from WCFGs by standard preprocessing methods (e.g.,
Stolcke 1995
and
Opedal et al. 2023
).
However, we note that eliminating such unary chain cycles does not produce an
\mathcal{N}
-bijectively equivalent grammar as infinitely many derivations are mapped to a single one that accounts for the total weight of all of them.

Nullary rules.

We also note that the case where the
\mathfrak{G}
may derive
\varepsilon
as its leftmost constituent also poses a challenge for top-down parsers. For example, that would be the case in
fig.

1
if
\mathrm{PossP}\rightarrow\mathrm{NP}\ \mathrm{POS}
was replaced by
\mathrm{PossP}\rightarrow\mathrm{X}\ \mathrm{NP}\ \mathrm{POS}
and
\mathrm{X}\rightarrow\varepsilon
; this grammar is not left-recursive, but the subgoal of recognizing an
\mathrm{NP}
in top-down parser will still, unfortunately, lead to infinite recursion. Thus, a complete solution to transforming a grammar into a top-down-parser-friendly grammar should also treat these cases. To that end, we can transform the original grammar into an equivalent nullary-free version with standard methods
(
Opedal et al. 2023
, §F)
before applying our GLCT-based left-recursion elimination recipe. As with unary rule elimination, nullary rule elimination does not produce an
\mathcal{N}
-bijectively equivalent grammar.

4.1
Experiments

In this section, we investigate how much the grammar size grows in practice when our GLCT recipe is used to eliminate left recursion. We compare our results to SLCT with top-down factoring (
section

3.1
) to see whether the additional degree of freedom given by
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
leads to any reduction in size.
We apply both transformations to nine grammars of different languages: Basque, English, French, German, Hebrew, Hungarian, Korean, Polish, and Swedish.
We use the ATIS grammar
(
Dahl et al. 1994
)
as our English grammar.
28
28

28

We selected the (boolean-weighted) ATIS grammar because it was used in prior work
(
Moore 2000
)
. We note, however, that—despite our best efforts—we were unable to replicate
Moore 2000
’s (
Moore 2000
)
exact
grammar size on it.

We derived the other grammars from the SPMRL 2013/2014 shared tasks treebanks
(
Seddah et al. 2013
;
Seddah et al. 2014
)
.
29
29

29

Specifically, we load all trees from the SPMRL 5k training dataset, delete the morphological annotations, collapse unary chains like
\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}\to\mathrm{Z}\to\boldsymbol{\alpha}
into
\mathrm{X}\to\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, and create a grammar from the remaining rules. The weights of the SPMRL grammars are set using maximum-likelihood estimation. None of the treebanks contained nullary rules.

Table 1:
Results of applying GLCT and SLCT on the ATIS and SPMRL grammars broken down by each of the nine languages. We present the resulting size in the raw output grammar (Raw), trimming (+Trim), and binarization plus nullary removal (
-\varepsilon
’s).

Experimental setup.

For GLCT, we set
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
according to our recipe. For SLCT, we set
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
according to
theorem

4
’s conditions for removing left-recursion.
We compare the grammar size and the number of rules of the raw output grammar to those of the input grammar. However, the raw output sizes can be reduced using useless rule filters (discussed in
section

3.1
and
appendix

G
), so we additionally apply trimming to the output grammars. When parsing, it is often practical to first binarize the grammar and remove nullary rules, so we perform those postprocessing steps as well.

As a sanity check, we verify that left recursion is removed in all settings by checking that the left-recursion graph of the output grammar is acyclic.
We present the results as evaluated on grammar size in
table

1
.

appendix

F
provides further results in terms of the number of rules.

Discussion.

Interestingly, the increase in size compared to the input grammar varies a lot between languages.
Previous work
(
Johnson and Roark 2000
;
Moore 2000
)
only evaluated on English and thus appear to have underestimated the blow-up caused by the left-corner transformation when applied to natural language grammars. Compare, for instance, the ratio between the trimmed size and the original size in
table

1
of English (
1.2
) to Basque (
3.4
), Hebrew (
6.7
), and Swedish (
23.7
).
By
proposition

3
, the number of rules in the output grammar scales with
|\overline{\mathcal{R}}|
, which by
theorem

4
is set as the left-recursive rules.

The GLCT produces smaller grammars than the SLCT for all languages before either of the postprocessing steps. This difference is (almost) eliminated post-trimming, however, which is unsurprising given that SLCT is a special case of GLCT (
section

3.1
). The small difference in size after trimming happens since two rules of the form
\mathrm{X}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\ \nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}

eq.

8b
and
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\rightarrow\varepsilon

eq.

8c
in SLCT are replaced by one rule
\mathrm{X}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}

eq.

8a
in GLCT. However, this difference disappears after nullary removal.

5
Conclusion

This work generalized the left-corner transformation to operate not only on a subset of rules but also on a subset of nonterminals. We achieve this by adapting frozen nonterminals from the speculation transformation. We exposed a tight connection between generalized left-corner transformation and speculation (
theorem

2
). Finally, and importantly, we proved the transformation’s correctness (
theorem

1
) and provided precise sufficient conditions for when it eliminates left recursion (
theorem

4
).

Limitations

Parsing runs in time proportional to the grammar size.
section

4.1
shows that we obtain the same grammar size after postprocessing from our method and the selective left-corner transformation, which gave us no reason to provide an empirical comparison on parsing runtime.

Moreover, it is thus of practical importance to restrict the growth of the grammar constant.
We have discussed a theoretical bound for grammar growth in
proposition

3
, investigated it empirically in
section

4.1
, and provided further tricks to reduce it in
appendix

G
.
Orthogonally to the left-corner transformation itself, it is possible to factor the grammar so that the grammar size is minimally affected by the transformation. Intuitively, reducing the number of left-recursive rules in
\mathcal{R}
will also reduce the number of rules that are required in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, which, in turn, leads to fewer rules in the output grammar. We did not present any such preprocessing techniques here, but
Moore 2000
provides a reference for two methods: left-factoring and non-left-recursive grouping.

Johnson and Roark 2000
give a second factoring trick in addition to top-down factoring (see
footnote

16
), which is similar to
Moore 2000
’s (
Moore 2000
) left-factoring.
We also mention that
Vieira 2023
’s (
Vieira 2023
) search-based technique for optimizing weighted logic programs could be directly applied to grammars. In particular, the search over sequences of define–unfold–fold transformations can be used to find a smaller grammar that encodes the same weighted language.

There appear to be connections between our notion of slashed nonterminals and the left quotient of formal languages that we did not explore in this paper.
30
30

30

The left quotient of a WCFG by a weighted regular language can be represented as another WCFG using a modified intersection construction
(
Bar-Hillel et al. 1961
;
Pasti et al. 2023
)
—see
Li et al. 2022
for details.
For example, the simplest case of the left quotient is the
Brzozowski 1964
derivative. The Brzozowski derivative of
\mathfrak{G}
with respect to
a\in\mathcal{V}
is equal to the weighted language of
\nicefrac{{\mathcal{S}}}{{a}}
in the output grammar
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
produced by speculation or GLCT, provided that the
\mathfrak{G}
is nullary-free,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\!=\!\mathcal{R}
, and
a\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. We suspect that other interesting connections are worth formalizing and exploring further.

Finally, we note that we could extend our transformation to deal with nullary rules directly rather than eliminating them by preprocessing (as discussed in
section

4
). The idea is to modify
eq.

8d
in GLCT so that the slashed nonterminal on its right-hand side is formed from the leftmost nonterminal that derives something
other than

\varepsilon
, rather than the leftmost symbol. For this extension to work out, we require that the grammar is preprocessed such that each nonterminal is replaced by two versions: one that generates only
\varepsilon
, and one that generates anything else.
Preprocessing the grammar in this way is also done in nullary rule elimination (see
Opedal et al. 2023
for details).

Ethical Statement

We do not foresee any ethical issues with our work.

Appendix A
Proof of
proposition

1
(Decomposition)

See
1

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\mathrm{X}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{X}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm/glct-equivalence}}]}

(13)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{K})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}w\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\beta_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\beta_{K}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\text{by Eq.~\ref{eq:weighted-language-equations}}]}

(14)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\mathrm{X}})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\textbf{1}\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\alpha}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\textbf{1}\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def/glct}}]}

(15)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\circ\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def/glct} and algebra}]}

(16)

Note that
eq.

15
specializes the sum to the only kinds of rules that can build
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}
: rules
eq.

8a
and
eq.

8b
.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of
proposition

2
(Weighted Language Relationship)

Lemma 1
.

\forall\alpha\in\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}
, there exists a weight- and yield- preserving bijection between
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}/\bot
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{\alpha}}
.

where the function
{\phi}
is
algorithm

1
. Call the function that maps the left tree to the right one
\psi
; all it does is freeze the spine and then call
{\phi}
on the
\boldsymbol{\beta}
subtrees. Recall that
theorem

1
established that
{\phi}
is a
\mathcal{N}
-structure-preserving bijection. Thus, it is straightforward to see that
\psi
is also weight- and yield-preserving bijection, as its inverse
\psi^{-1}
undoes these steps and calls
{\phi^{-1}}
on the subtrees. This proves
lemma

1
.
∎

Lemma 2
.

\forall\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N},\alpha\in\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}
, there exists a weight- and yield-preserving bijection between
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{X}}/\alpha
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}}
.

Proof (Sketch).

On the left, we have a prototypical derivation from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{X}}/\alpha
with its spine exposed. Recall that these trees result from replacing the left corner, which is why there is an
\varepsilon
. Here
\mathrm{X}=\gamma^{(K)}
and
\alpha=\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}
. On the right, we have its corresponding derivation in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}}
.

Appendix C
Proof of
theorem

1
(Bijective Equivalence)

Roadmap.

We will show that
\mathfrak{G}\equiv_{\mathcal{N}}\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
for any choice of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Our proof makes use of two lemmas,
lemma

3
and
lemma

4
, to establish that the derivation mapping
{\phi}
(
algorithm

1
)
and its inverse
{\phi^{-1}}
(
algorithm

2
)
define a bijection of the necessary type.

lemma

3
shows that
{\phi}
preserves label, weight, and yield.

lemma

4
shows that
{\phi}
is invertible and, thus, that a bijection exists.

Lemma 3
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}\!=\!\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R},w\right\rangle
be a WCFG.
Let
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
where
\overline{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq(\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V})
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
.
Then,
algorithm

1
defines a function
{\phi}
that is

(i)

type
{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

(ii)

label-preserving

(iii)

yield-preserving

(iv)

weight-preserving

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}=\left\langle\mathcal{N}^{\prime},\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right\rangle
. Note: by construction (
3
),
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{N}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}
.

For notational convenience, we extend
\omega
and
\sigma
to apply to a sequence of derivations

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

It is straightforward to verify that
{\phi}
satisfies properties (i–iv) for sequences of derivations; for brevity, we will not do so.

Fix an arbitrary derivation tree
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}
. Our proof will proceed by structural induction on the
subtree relation
:
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\prec\boldsymbol{\delta}\Longleftrightarrow\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\text{ is a (strict) subtree of }\boldsymbol{\delta}
. It is well-known that
\prec
is well-founded ordering, making it suitable for induction (e.g.,
Burstall 1969
).

Inductive hypothesis (IH)
:
\forall\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\prec\boldsymbol{\delta}
: properties (i–iv) hold.

Base case
(
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{V}
):
Here
{\phi}
is the identity mapping. Thus, properties (i–iv) are clearly preserved.

Inductive case
(
\boldsymbol{\delta}\notin\mathcal{V}
): Here
\boldsymbol{\delta}
must have the general form:

\gamma^{(K)}
\gamma^{(K-1)}

\gamma^{(2)}
\gamma^{(1)}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-2})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})}
ab
\cdots

where
K\geq 1
,
\forall i\in\{1,\mdots@,K\}\colon\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i})}\in(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N})^{*}
, and

(\gamma^{(K)}\to\gamma^{(K-1)}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})})\cdots(\gamma^{(2)}\to\gamma^{(1)}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})})
is
\zeta\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
,

the spine of the
\boldsymbol{\delta}
.

Observation
1
: The subtree
\gamma^{(1)}
ab

is visually similar to the “kink” in the transformed tree that is determined by

in the case where

.
More formally, let
{\widehat{k}}\!=\!\min(\left\{i\mid\gamma^{(i)}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}},i\in\left\{1,\ldots K\right\}\right\})

. The cases correspond to
\lambda\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\neq\bot

We check each of the properties:

(i)

{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
: In this case, we can check that
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
by verifying that each rule used in the definition of
{\phi}
appears in
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
, and then invoking the inductive hypothesis on the subtrees.

It is straightforward to verify that the mapping
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
preserves weight and that the tree exists in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
by crosschecking each of the rules that appear in it with the GLCT construction (
3
):

•

The top rule
\gamma^{(K)}\to\widetilde{\gamma^{(K)}}
is an instance of
eq.

8a
.
The side condition
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is satisfied by
2
(
\forall i\in\left\{1,\ldots,K\right\}:\gamma^{(i)}\notin\overline{\mathcal{C}}
).

•

Each middle rule
{\widetilde{\gamma^{(i)}}\to\widetilde{\gamma^{(i-1)}}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i-1})}}
is an instance of
eq.

8f
.
The side condition
\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\mathrm{X}_{1}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathrm{X}_{1}\notin\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is satisfied in each case because spine guarantees inclusion in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, and by
2
(
\forall i\in\left\{1,\ldots,K\right\}:\gamma^{(i)}\notin\overline{\mathcal{C}}
).

•

The bottom rule
{\widetilde{\gamma^{(1)}}\to\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({0})}}
is an instance of
eq.

8e
. The side condition
{\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\beta}\notin\overline{\mathcal{R}}}
is satisfied by
1
.
32
32

32

Recall that if
\gamma^{(1)}\in\mathcal{V}
, then
\widetilde{\gamma^{(1)}}=\gamma^{(1)}
.

Since we have verified that each of the rules in the construction of
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
are in
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
, and the subtrees are in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
by IH, then it follows that
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

label-preserving: It is clear by inspection that
\boldsymbol{\delta}
and
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
have the same root label.

In this case, we observe
3
:
\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,{\widehat{k}}\!-\!1\}:\gamma^{(i)}\notin\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

We check each of the properties:

(i)

{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
: We verify that the rules in the derivation
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
are in
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
:

•

The top rule
{\gamma^{(K)}\to\widetilde{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}}\;\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}}}}
is an instance of
eq.

8b
.
The side condition
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N},\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}
holds because the definition of
{\widehat{k}}
ensures that
\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.

•

The rules in the left subtree follow the same argument as the proof of the
{\widehat{k}}=\infty
case.

•

Each middle rule of the right subtree

{\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{(i-1)}}}\to\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i-1})}\;\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{(i)}}}}
(for
i\!=\!{\widehat{k}}{+}1,\ldots,K
) is an instance of
eq.

8d
. The side condition
\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\mathrm{X}_{1}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathrm{Y}\in\mathcal{N}
hold because
\gamma^{(K)}\in\mathcal{N}
, and each of the
\gamma^{(i)}\to\gamma^{(i-1)}\;\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i-1})}
rules was part of the spine (thus, in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
).

•

The bottom rule of the right subtree
{\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{(K)}}}\to\varepsilon}
is an instance of
eq.

8f
.

Lastly, because each
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i})}
subtree is in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
by IH, we have that
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

root-preserving: It is clear by inspection that
\boldsymbol{\delta}
and
{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
have the same root label.

(iii)

yield-preserving: The argument here is essentially the same as the
{\widehat{k}}=\infty
case. The only difference is that the empty string (
\varepsilon
) will be concatenated as the right-most element, which does not change the yield as
\varepsilon
is the identity element of the string-concatenation operator.

(iv)

weight-preserving: The argument is similar to the
{\widehat{k}}=\infty
case

\displaystyle\omega\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)

We have used the specific weight settings and the fact that
1
is the identity element of the
\otimes
-operator.

Conclusion.

We have successfully verified that the properties (i–iv) hold in each possible case; thus, by the principle of induction,
lemma

3
is true.
∎

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}=\left\langle\mathcal{N},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\right\rangle
,

\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}=\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})

for any choice of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
.
Then, the functions
{\phi}
and
{\phi^{-1}}
defined by
algorithm

1
and
algorithm

2
(respectively) are inverses of each other.
Thus, a bijection of type
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
exists.

Proof.

N.B.

We first explain why only a bijection of type
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
rather than a bijection of type
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. The reason is that the set
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
contains derivations rooted at the new slashed and frozen nonterminals, which do not exist in
\mathfrak{G}
. However, all derivations with a root label in
\mathcal{N}
are preserved in the bijection; that is why we restricted the type of the bijection to have the range
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
.
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
\mathcal{D}
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
{\phi}
{\phi^{-1}}

Note that it would be sufficient to show a bijection between
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}}
, but we prove the stronger version as it fits with our inductive proof strategy. We note that the derivations in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
are each labeled by either a frozen or slashed nonterminals symbol. The meanings of these frozen and slashed symbols do not need to be preserved since they are undefined in
\mathfrak{G}
. What is important is that the derivations in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
that use these derivations use them in a meaning-preserving manner.

Our proof will refer to the explicit functions
{\phi}
and
{\phi^{-1}}
(
algorithm

1
and
algorithm

2
).
We will prove that
{\phi}
and
{\phi^{-1}}
are inverses of each other in two parts:

•

Part 1
:
We will show that
\forall\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}\colon{\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
.

•

Part 2
:
We will show that
\forall\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}\colon{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}
.

Proof of Part 1:

Our proof will proceed by structural induction on the
subtree relation
(
\prec
). Fix an arbitrary derivation tree
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Inductive hypothesis (IH):

For all subtrees
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime\prime}}}\prec\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
where
\ell\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime\prime}}}\right)\in\mathcal{N}
:

{\phi}({\phi^{-1}}(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime\prime}}}))=\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime\prime}}}
.

Base case (
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\in\mathcal{V}
)
:

{\phi}
and
{\phi^{-1}}
are the identity function; thus,
{\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
as required.

Sequence case:

Both
{\phi}
and
{\phi^{-1}}
apply pointwise to sequences; thus,

{\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{1}}^{\prime}}}\cdots\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{K}}^{\prime}}}\right)\right)={\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{1}}^{\prime}}}\right)\cdots{\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{K}}^{\prime}}}\right)\right)={\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{1}}^{\prime}}}\right)\right)\cdots{\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{K}}^{\prime}}}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{1}}^{\prime}}}\cdots\boldsymbol{{\delta_{{K}}^{\prime}}}
as required.

Inductive case
: There are two cases to consider:
(1) the top rule is an instance of
eq.

8b
or
(2) the top rule is an instance of
eq.

8a
. These cases are mutually exclusive, as a difference in the top rule ensures any pair of derivations are unequal. We can see that these cases are exhaustive, as the only way to have a derivation labeled
\mathcal{N}
that in
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is if the top rule of
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
is an instance of
eq.

8b
or
eq.

8a
.

•

Case (top rule is
eq.

8a
)
:
In this case,
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
must have the following form:

\gamma^{(K)}
\widetilde{\gamma^{(K)}}

\widetilde{\gamma^{(K-1)}}

\widetilde{\gamma^{(2)}}

\widetilde{\gamma^{(1)}}

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({0})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-2})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})}
ab
\cdots

We note that the chain of rules exposed along the left edge must have the following structure: we start with an instance of
eq.

8a
followed by zero or more instances of
eq.

8f
ending in an instance of
eq.

8e
. None of the
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i})}
-subtrees contain slashed or frozen symbols in their labels. However, note that the
subtrees
of the
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i})}
-subtrees may contain slashed or frozen symbols.

We verify that
{\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
below:

\displaystyle{\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\right)={\phi}\left(\!\!\!\hbox to143.33pt{\vbox to243.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 97.16597pt\lower-234.28912pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The first step applies
algorithm

2
, and the second step applies
algorithm

1
.
By induction, each recursive call in this expression successfully inverts
{\phi}
for their respective argument. It is also clear, by inspection, that

10
of
algorithm

1

correctly inverts the transformation of the spine performed on

9
of
algorithm

2
.
Thus, by induction, our definition of the inverse is correct for this case.

•

Case (top rule is
eq.

8b
)
: In this case,
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
must have the following form:

\gamma^{(K)}
\widetilde{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}}

\widetilde{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}}-1)}}

\widetilde{\gamma^{(2)}}

\widetilde{\gamma^{(1)}}

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({0})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({{\widehat{k}}-2})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({{\widehat{k}}-1})}
ab
\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}}}

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({{\widehat{k}}})}
ab
\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}}+1)}}}

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({{\widehat{k}}+1})}
ab
\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{(K-2)}}}
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-2})}
ab
\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{(K-1)}}}
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})}
ab
\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{(K)}}}

\varepsilon
\cdots
\cdots

Here, the top rule is an instance of
eq.

8b
; thus, it combines a derivation labeled by some frozen nonterminal
\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}
with a derivation of some slashed nonterminal
\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}}}
. Notice that the attached frozen nonterminals
\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}
must match the slashed nonterminal’s denominator, and the slashed nonterminal’s numerator must match the label
\ell\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\right)
. The left subtree exposes the chain of rules that built the frozen nonterminal
\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}
. This chain is a (possibly empty) sequence of
eq.

8f
followed by the base case
eq.

8e
. The right subtree exposes the chain of rules that built slashed nonterminal
\nicefrac{{\gamma^{(K)}}}{{\gamma^{({\widehat{k}})}}}
. These are a (possibly empty) sequence of
eq.

8d
ending in an instance of the base case
eq.

8c
. By construction, none of the
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i})}
-subtrees may contain slashed or frozen symbols in their root labels, as they must be instances of one of the rules
eq.

8c
,
eq.

8d
,
eq.

8e
, or
eq.

8f
.

{\phi}\left({\phi^{-1}}\left(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
by applying

algorithm

2

and

1
in the equations below:

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.75919}{0.65088}{-0.65088}{0.75919}{-126.41487pt}{-81.1638pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}

By induction, each recursive call in this expression successfully inverts
{\phi}
for its respective argument. It is also clear, by inspection, that
12
of
algorithm

1
correctly inverts the transformation of the spine performed on
7
of
algorithm

2
. Thus, by induction, our definition of the inverse is correct for this case.

Conclusion (Part 1).

We have verified the two cases of the inductive step; thus, by the principle of induction, we have shown that Part 1 of
lemma

4
holds.

We now continue to Part 2 (next page).

Proof of Part 2:

Our proof will proceed by structural induction on the
subtree relation
(
\prec
). Fix an arbitrary derivation tree
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}
.

Inductive hypothesis (IH):

For all subtrees
\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\prec\boldsymbol{\delta}
:
{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}
.

Base case (
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{V}
)
:

{\phi}
and
{\phi^{-1}}
are the identity function; thus,
{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}
as required.

Sequence case:

Both
{\phi}
and
{\phi^{-1}}
apply pointwise to sequences; thus,

{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\delta}_{K}\right)\right)={\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\right)\cdots{\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{K}\right)\right)={\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\right)\right)\cdots{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{K}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\delta}_{K}
as required.

Inductive case
: Recall from the proof of the inductive case of
lemma

3
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}
must have the general form:

\gamma^{(K)}
\gamma^{(K-1)}

\gamma^{(2)}
\gamma^{(1)}

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({0})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-2})}
ab
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})}
ab
\cdots

where
K\geq 1
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({i})}\in(\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{N})^{*},\forall i\{1,\mdots@,K\}
, and
(\gamma^{(K)}\to\gamma^{(K-1)}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({K-1})})\cdots(\gamma^{(2)}\to\gamma^{(1)}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{({1})})
is its spine
\zeta\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
. (We only show the cases for when
\gamma^{(1)}\in\mathcal{N}
. The case when
\gamma^{(1)}\in\mathcal{V}
follows the same argument.)

Now, let
{\widehat{k}}
be the index of the left corner, or
\infty
if it does not exist. We consider two cases:
{\widehat{k}}=\infty
and
{\widehat{k}}<\infty
.

Case
(
{\widehat{k}}=\infty
): We seek to show that
{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}
for this case. The equations below depict how spine transformations performed by

algorithm

1
and

algorithm

2
cancel each other out:

Thus, by induction,
{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}
for all derivations

that fall into this case.
