\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\delta}

Thus, by induction,
{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}
for all derivations
\boldsymbol{\delta}
that fall into this case.

Conclusion (Part 2).

Since we have shown that each of the possible cases (
{\widehat{k}}=\infty
and
{\widehat{k}}<\infty
) satisfy
{\phi^{-1}}\left({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{\delta}
, it follows that Part 2 of
lemma

4
is true.

Conclusion.

We have proven Part 1 and Part 2; thus,
lemma

4
holds.
∎

See
1

Proof.

Recall from
section

2
that
\mathcal{N}
-bijective equivalence (
\mathfrak{G}\equiv_{\mathcal{N}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
) requires the existence of a structure-preserving bijective mapping of type
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
.

lemma

3
shows that
{\phi}
in
algorithm

1
is a mapping of type
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
that preserves the desired structure (label, weight, and yield). Thus,
{\phi}
is structure-preserving.

lemma

4
shows that
{\phi}
is a bijection of
{\phi}\colon\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
. Thus, we have verified the existence of a structure-preserving bijection and, therefore,
\mathfrak{G}\equiv_{\mathcal{N}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
.
∎

Appendix D
Proof (Sketch) of
theorem

2
(Speculation–GLCT Bijective Equivalence)

See
2

Proof (Sketch).

A straightforward inductive argument can be made to show that the derivations of speculation and GLCT under the conditions of
theorem

2
will produce isomorphic trees.

We sketch the derivation mapping from speculation to GLCT. We do not only provide a sketch for the fact that the mapping is a structure-preserving bijection. It is straightforward, albeit laborious, to extend our proof sketch to a proof; such a proof would follow the same structure as our proof of
theorem

1
.

Base case (terminals).

This case is trivial, as terminals are identical between the transformations.

Inductive Case.

We consider three cases for each kind of nonterminal: original, frozen, and slashed. In the diagrams below, we show how the backbone of the derivation is changed. We note that the
\boldsymbol{\beta}
-subtrees of the tree are transformed recursively. In each of the diagrams below, the speculation derivation is on the left, and its corresponding GLCT derivation is on the right.

Original nonterminals.
This case was discussed in
fig.

2
.

Frozen nonterminals.

The rules defining the frozen nonterminals are identical between the transformations, so the backbone is unchanged in this case.

Slashed nonterminals.

Recall from
section

3.2
that the difference between the slashed nonterminals in speculation and GLCT is the recursive rule defining slashed nonterminals (
eq.

11d
vs.
eq.

8d
). The only effect of this difference is that slashed nonterminals in speculation have left-branching derivations, and in GLCT, they have right-branching derivations. The mapping below works for any slashed nonterminal:

\displaystyle\hbox to163.08pt{\vbox to133.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 120.25713pt\lower-124.4799pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}{{{
{}{}{}}}}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 75.27 -59.12 L 65.61 -50.21 L 55.29 -61.39 L 64.95 -70.31 Z M 55.29 -61.39}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.73483}{-0.67824}{0.67824}{0.73483}{42.72658pt}{-42.84087pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Structure-preservation and invertibility.

In each of the cases above, it is straightforward to see that mapping is

•

label-preserving: the trees in the diagrams each have the same label.

•

yield-preserving: the yield of each
\boldsymbol{\beta}
-subtree is preserved (by induction hypothesis), and string concatenation is associative with identity element
\varepsilon
.

•

weight-preserving: the weights of rule weights multiplied are equal (by construction),
\otimes
is associative and commutative (by assumption), and each subtree is equally weighted (by induction hypothesis).

•

invertible: the mapping between the trees is invertible because the manipulation of its backbone (i.e., its exposed structure) is invertible, and the mapping for each subtree is invertible (by induction hypothesis).

Conclusion.

The above cases cover all possibilities. Each sketches the structure-preserving bijective mapping between speculation and GLCT derivations for all their nonterminals (original, slashed, and frozen). Therefore, we have sketched a proof of
theorem

2
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of
theorem

4
(Left-Recursion Elimination)

See
4

Proof.

Recall from
theorem

1
that there exists a structure-preserving bijection between
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
. Our proof shows how the mapping
{\phi}
(
algorithm

1
) removes (unbounded) left recursion from
\mathfrak{G}
by analyzing the structure of its transformed trees. Below, we show how
{\phi}
takes any derivation
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}
(
left
) and maps a tree
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}={\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right)
where
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
(
right
).

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}{}^{(K)}_{\text{bot}}
is a (possibly empty) sequence of terminals. We note that the
\triangle
-subtrees on the right are isomorphic to their counterparts on the left because
{\phi}
has (implicitly) transformed them.

Let
\mathcal{C}{}^{(1)},\mathcal{C}{}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathcal{C}{}^{(K)}
denote the segmentation of the left edge of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
into a sequence of spines. The node names indicate its top and bottom elements. Note that spines may contain a single node;
\gamma{}^{(i)}_{\text{top}}\!=\!\gamma{}^{(i)}_{\text{bot}}
in these cases. We also note that the transitions between spines occur when a rule on the left is not in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Avoiding a subtle mistake.

We may assume that each
\triangle
-subtree is non-empty because the
\mathfrak{G}
is unary-free by assumption. This unary-free assumption is important because it ensures that the form of
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
will not be left-recursive simply because some of its
\triangle
-subtrees are empty.

The accordion effect.

Recall that
{\phi}
(recursively) hoists each left-corner subtree along its respective spine.
33
33

33

This interpretation is discussed throughout the paper: informally in
fig.

1
and
section

3.1
, semi-formally in
fig.

2
, and formally in the proof of
theorem

1
(
appendix

C
).

The accordion effect is the result of repeatedly hoisting: Each spine is contracted into two nodes on the left side of
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
corresponding to their respective spine’s top and bottom elements. Each of the top nonterminals has an unbounded chain of right-recursive rules corresponding to the extracted spine.

The accordion effect assumes that the bottommost node of the spine is always
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
-labeled and, thus, is hoisted by
{\phi}
as in the diagram. It is easy to see that our condition on
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
guarantees this because every node that might end the spine is included in
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
(by eq.
section

4
).
Furthermore, if
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is a superset of
\mathrm{bottoms}(\overline{\mathcal{R}})
, then
{\phi}
(by definition) will be unaffected because the bottommost elements of the spines (i.e., those that are hoisted) are already included.
34
34

34

We note that other ways exist to set
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
that can break left-recursion cycles. For example, if
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
is a feedback node set (i.e., a subset of nodes such that removing them and their incoming and outgoing edges results in an acyclic graph). However, the construction is messier, as there will be instances of
eq.

8f
along the left of
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
. This still eliminates left recursion, as those frozen nonterminals will not be left recursive. Additionally, it is unclear whether there is any benefit in terms of grammar size or left-recursion depth to these alternatives; thus, we only analyze the simpler case.

For simplicity, the accordion diagram only illustrates the case where each spine
\mathcal{C}{}^{(i)}
is not a singleton containing one element outside of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
. We now address that case. Here,
\gamma{}^{(i)}_{\text{top}}\!=\!\gamma{}^{(i)}_{\text{bot}}
and the rule
\gamma{}^{(i)}_{\text{top}}\rightarrow\gamma{}^{(i+1)}_{\text{top}}\;\boldsymbol{\beta}{}^{(i)}_{\text{top}}
cannot be in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Like the case depicted, the spine contracts into two nodes on the right:
\gamma{}^{(i)}_{\text{top}}
and
\widetilde{\gamma{}^{(i)}_{\text{top}}}
. However, we now have an instance of
eq.

8a
instead of
eq.

8b
in the tree on the right; thus, there is no slashed chain of nonterminals.

Bounding the left-recursion depth.

The result of the accordion effect is that each spine on the left (of unbounded length) is contracted into two nodes in the tree on the right. From here, we can see that
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
has bounded left-recursive depth as long as there is a bounded number of spines. Recall that each spine is the maximum-length sequence of rules along the left of the derivation starting at the root. Since
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
includes all left-recursive rules, this means that each spine in the sequence
\mathcal{C}{}^{(1)},\mathcal{C}{}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathcal{C}{}^{(K)}

completes
at least one SCC—meaning its nodes cannot be visited later in the path. Thus,
d(\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}})\leq 2\!\cdot\!C
where
C
is the number of SCCs in the left-recursion graph
G
for
\mathfrak{G}
.
35
35

35

This bound can be reduced by including more rules in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
at the cost of increasing the size of
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
. For example, if
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\!=\!\mathcal{R}
, the left-recursion depth is two, but
\mathfrak{G}
will likely be larger according to
proposition

3
.

Conclusion.

The above argument bounds
d({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right))
as a function of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
. We can bound
d(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime})
by
\max_{\boldsymbol{\delta}\in\mathcal{D}}d({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right))
because
\boldsymbol{\delta}
and
\boldsymbol{{\delta^{\prime}}}
are in one-to-one correspondence and, thus, no trees in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}
will be overlooked. Note that
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is trimmed so we can focus on
\mathcal{N}
-derivations. Because our earlier bound,
d({\phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\delta}\right))\leq 2\!\cdot\!C
, is independent of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
, the maximization is trivial:
d(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime})\leq 2\!\cdot\!C
. Thus, the left-recursion in
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is bounded, and
theorem

4
holds.
∎

Appendix G
Filtering Optimization for GLCT

This section shows how the filtering tricks mentioned in
section

3.1
can be incorporated into the left-corner transformation to reduce the number of useless rules created by
3
.
36
36

36

Baars et al. 2010
adopt similar filtering techniques.

Our first filtering trick is based on
Johnson 1998
’s (
Johnson 1998
) strategy for reducing the number of useless rules. It works as follows: the slashed nonterminal
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}
is useless if
\mathrm{X}
is not reachable from
\mathrm{Y}
in a left-recursion graph for
\mathfrak{G}
which only includes the rule set
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Our second filtering trick is based on
Moore 2000
’s (
Moore 2000
) filtering strategy, which extends
Johnson 1998
’s (
Johnson 1998
) with additional filtering based on retained nonterminals. The slashed nonterminal
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}
is useless if none of the following hold:

(i)

\mathrm{Y}\!=\!\mathcal{S}
,

(ii)

\mathrm{Y}
appears on the right-hand side of some rule in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
in a position
other than
the leftmost position,

(iii)

\mathrm{Y}
appears on the right-hand side of some rule in
\mathcal{R}{\setminus}\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

More formally, the set of
retained nonterminals
is

\displaystyle R\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{def}}}{{=}}}(

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{S}\}\cup\{\beta_{i}\mid(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta})\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\beta_{i}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}\}\cup\{\beta_{i}\mid(\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{}\boldsymbol{\beta})\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\overline{\mathcal{R}},\beta_{i}\in\boldsymbol{\beta}\})

(17)

Let
\mathrm{X}\rightsquigarrow\alpha
denote whether
\alpha
is reachable from
\mathrm{X}
in the left-recursion graph for
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. Let
\mathrm{X}\rightsquigarrow\overline{\mathcal{C}}
denote whether
\exists\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}\colon\mathrm{X}\rightsquigarrow\alpha
.
Then, we use the following equations for the rules
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in R\!\setminus\!\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(18a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\widetilde{\alpha}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\alpha}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in R,\mathrm{X}\rightsquigarrow\alpha,{\alpha\in\overline{\mathcal{C}}}

(18b)

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\xrightarrow{\textbf{1}}\varepsilon\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\in R,\mathrm{X}\rightsquigarrow\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(18c)

\displaystyle\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\beta}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{Y}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\mathrm{Y}\in R,\mathrm{Y}\rightsquigarrow\alpha,\alpha\rightsquigarrow\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(18d)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{R}\!\setminus\!\overline{\mathcal{R}}

(18e)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{X}}\xrightarrow{w}\widetilde{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}\xrightarrow{w}\alpha\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}},\alpha\notin\overline{\mathcal{C}}

(18f)

Verifying that these filters can only drop useless rules is straightforward.

Appendix H
Fast Nullary Elimination

Opedal et al. 2023
gives an approach for eliminating nullary rules from a grammar
\mathfrak{G}
that requires computing the
null weight

\mathfrak{G}_{\mathrm{X}}(\varepsilon)
of each nonterminal (
\mathrm{X}\in\mathcal{N}
). These null weights are the (smallest) solution to the system of polynomial equations given by
eq.

7
where we replace
1
with
0
in the base case
eq.

7a
.
In general, this system can be solved numerically using fixed-point iteration or Newton’s method (see, e.g.,
Esparza et al. 2007
;
Nederhof and Satta 2008
;
Vieira 2023
).
In our case, where
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is the output of GLCT, we can solve the system exactly and efficiently, assuming the original grammar
\mathfrak{G}
is free of nullary rules.
37
37

37

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}(\varepsilon)\!=\!w
. If
w\!\neq\!\textbf{0}
, we require at least one nullary rule of the form
\mathcal{S}\xrightarrow{w}\varepsilon
. This limited type of rule is straightforward to accommodate in our approach, but we have omitted it from this discussion for simplicity.

Specifically, this system of equations for
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is
linear
and can be solved exactly in
\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathcal{N}|^{3}\right)
time by using an algorithm for the algebraic path problem (e.g.,
Lehmann 1977
;
Tarjan 1981
) instead of relying on numerical-approximation methods.

Proposition 4

(Fast null-weight computation)
.

The null-weight equations for
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\!=\!\textsc{glct}(\mathfrak{G},\overline{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathcal{C}})
are linear, provided
\mathfrak{G}
is nullary free.

Proof.

First, we observe that only the nullary rules are those with slashed nonterminal on its left-hand side,
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\rightarrow\varepsilon

eq.

8c
. We can see that rules created by
eq.

8d
and
eq.

8b
dictate that these slashed nonterminals only occur on the right-corner position of other rules. It then follows that any derivation with an
\varepsilon
-yield contains at most one nullary rule
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\rightarrow\varepsilon
as well as a (possibly zero) number of unary rules on the form
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\rightarrow\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}}
.
This is the case because rules of the form
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\beta}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}}
with
|\boldsymbol{\beta}|>0
contribute a null weight of
0
by the assumption that the original grammar has no nullary rules (i.e., each of the original symbols has a null weight of zero, and because GLCT is
\mathcal{N}
-bijectively equivalent, they continue to have a null weight of zero).

The null-weight equations simplify to the following system:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\xrightarrow{w}\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{K}\;\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\\
\end{subarray}}w\otimes\cancelto{\textbf{0}}{\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\beta_{1}}(\varepsilon)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\cancelto{\textbf{0}}{\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\beta_{K}}(\varepsilon)}\otimes\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}}}(\varepsilon)\quad\oplus\quad\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\xrightarrow{w}\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}w

(19)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\xrightarrow{w}\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}w\otimes\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}}}(\varepsilon)\quad\oplus\quad\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\xrightarrow{w}\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}w

(20)

We can represent this system as a linear equation. We define a matrix
\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{W}^{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}
and vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{W}^{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}
. For each
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}},\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}},\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
:

\mathbf{W}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}},\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Y}}}\xrightarrow{w}\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{Z}}})\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}w\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{v}_{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{X}}}{{\mathrm{X}}}\xrightarrow{w}\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}w

(21)

Then, the null weight for each
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
is

\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\varepsilon)=\left[\mathbf{W}^{*}\mathbf{v}\right]_{\alpha}

(22)

where
\mathbf{W}^{*}
is the solution to
\mathbf{W}^{*}\!=\!\mathbf{I}\oplus\mathbf{W}\,\mathbf{W}^{*}
. In the case of the real semiring
\mathbf{W}^{*}\!=\!(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{W})^{-1}
. For other semirings, it may be computed in
\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{V}|^{3}\right)
time using an algebraic path solver (e.g.,
Lehmann 1977
;
Tarjan 1981
).
∎
